- MaleCNS v1.0 CX core (verified in this repository, docs/CIRCUITS.md): EPG 46, PEN_a 20, PEN_b 22, PEG 18, Delta7 42, EL 18, ExR 26 = 148 neurons, 7,216 edges, 136,003 synaptic contacts. Neurotransmitters: EPG/PEN/PEG cholinergic (excitatory), Delta7 glutamatergic (inhibitory in fly).
- MaleCNS v1.0 fan-shaped-body vector computer, NEVER EXTRACTED in this repo (counted for this note from body-annotations-male-cns-v1.0-minconf-0.5.feather): PFN 456 neurons / 10 types (PFNa, PFNd, PFNm_a, PFNm_b, PFNp_a-d, PFNv, ...); hDelta 189 / 13 types (hDeltaA-M); vDelta 430 / 15; FC1-FC3 274 / 13 (incl. FC2A/B/C); FS 308 / 14; FR 36 / 2; PFL 50 / 3 (PFL1 12, PFL2 12, PFL3 24); PFR 44 / 2; PFGs 18 / 1; FB tangential FB1-FB9 601 / 166; ER 282 / 26; ExR 26 / 8; EL 18; LNO 8 / 3; LAL 665 / 204; DNa 52 / 25.
- MEASURED Fourier decomposition of the MaleCNS CX offset profiles (for this note). EPG->Delta7->EPG effective disynaptic inhibition: DC 902.1, first harmonic 943.8 at phase 180.0 deg, second 85.6, third 66.4, fourth 15.3; first-harmonic share of non-DC power 0.987; amplitude/DC = 1.046. EPG->Delta7 monosynaptic: DC 10.0, h1 10.8 at -177.4 deg, h1 share 0.985, amplitude/DC 1.078. EPG->EPG: DC 5.2, h1 9.3 at 0.0 deg, h1 share only 0.512. Delta7->EPG: h1 share 0.378. Delta7->Delta7: DC 14.9, h1 3.9 at 177.2 deg. PEN_a->EPG: DC 15.6, h1 10.4, h2 11.8 (two-lobed because both hemispheres are pooled).
- MEASURED PB-glomerulus -> FB-column offsets in MaleCNS v1.0 (for this note; fit col = a_L + glom on the left, col = a_R - glom on the right, irregular neurons excluded; n = left/right neurons). PFGs 8/8: a_L 0.00+-0.00, a_R 10.00+-0.00. PFR_a 12/12: 0.00/10.00. PFNd 20/20: -1.00+-0.00 / 10.00+-0.00. PFNa 29/27: -0.90+-0.31 / 10.89+-0.32. PFNv 10/10: -0.80+-0.42 / 10.80+-0.42. PFNm_a 9/12: -0.89 / 10.92. PFNm_b 12/14: -0.83 / 10.79. PFL1 6/6: +1.17+-0.41 / 8.83+-0.41. PFL2 6/6: +4.00+-0.00 / 6.00+-0.00. PFL3 10/10: +1.90+-0.32 / 8.00+-0.00.
- Left-vs-right phase splits derived from the above at 45 deg per FB column, referenced to the unshifted PFGs/PFR_a: PFNd 45.0 deg, PFNv 72.0 deg, PFNm_b 72.9 deg, PFNa 80.3 deg, PFNm_a 81.2 deg, PFL1 105.0 deg, PFL3 175.5 deg, PFL2 360.0 deg (= 0 mod 360, i.e. BOTH sides at 180 deg). Per-side half-splits: PFN types 22.5-40.6 deg (literature: 45 deg), PFL3 87.8 deg (literature: 67.5 deg), PFL2 180 deg (literature: 180 deg, EXACT MATCH). Quantisation floor is 45 deg because the column labels are integers.
- Literature phase offsets to target: PFN left/right FB columns shifted by ~+/-45 deg relative to EPG heading; hDelta project 180 deg (half the FB width); PFL3 heading input shifted ~+/-67.5 deg (1.5 columns); PFL2 shifted 180 deg (Hulse et al. 2021 eLife 10:e66039).
- PFL3 population preferred directions (Westeinde et al. 2024, via search, UNCERTAIN): PFL3L = (67.5, 22.5, -22.5, -22.5, -67.5, -112.5, -112.5, -157.5, 157.5, 157.5, 112.5, 67.5) deg; PFL3R = mirror image. 12 cells per side, 45 deg sampling, +/-67.5 deg global shift.
- CX gross anatomy (Hulse et al. 2021): EB 8 tiles / 16 wedges; PB 18 glomeruli (9 per side, 45 deg per glomerulus); FB ~9 columns x 9 layers; ~250 CX cell types. Comparative scale: Drosophila ~400 PFN vs bumblebee 854+ PFN, 188 hDelta, 69 EPG/PEG, ~40 PEN (Sayre et al. 2021 eLife 10:e68911).
- Kutschireiter et al. 2023 PNAS network implementation (from the repository's code): N_HD = 100 threshold-linear units; W_HD_HD[i,j] = (2/N)(o + max(0, w0 + w1 cos(phi_i - phi_j))) with w0 = 0, w1 = 2, o = -0.2; W_HD_AVplus[i,j] = (2/N) max(0, sin(phi_i - phi_j + pi/4)); W_HD_AVminus[i,j] = (2/N) max(0, -sin(phi_i - phi_j - pi/4)); W_Del7_HD[i,j] = (2/N)(0.5 - 0.5 cos(phi_i - phi_j)); W_Del7_Del7 = (2/N)(0.1); Delta7 tuning sigma = 0.1; kappa decoded as kappa = ||(2/N) sum_i r_i (cos phi_i, sin phi_i)||. Recurrent gain w_HD_HD = alpha_tilde + 1/(kappa_phi + kappa_y); AV coupling w = sqrt(2) kappa_y/(kappa_y + kappa_phi); kappa_0 = 10; 10,000 trajectories per configuration; kappa_z scanned 0.01-100.
- Kim et al. 2019 plasticity parameters (github.com/ModelDBRepository/261585, param_Plasticity.m): epsilon_W_input = 0.5 (0.75 for the 2-D input case), W_max = 0.33; three rules compared — 'no learning' (W_max = 0), 'SOM inhib, post-synaptically gated, input profile', 'Hebb inhib, pre-synaptically gated, wedge profile'.
- Dan et al. 2024 goal-learning model: 32 heading units, 16 basis functions, goal weight learning rate alphaW = 0.001, heading learning rate alphaH = 0.05 (50x separation), 9 trials x 240 s, saccade lognormal mean 3.89 / spread 0.54, saccade duration 320 ms, fixation threshold 0.79.
- Turner-Evans et al. 2017 P-EN physiology: single-cell angular-velocity modulation 5.6 +/- 3.7 Hz, bandwidth 145 +/- 82 deg/s; P-EN bump lags E-PG by ~1 glomerulus (~45 deg) in the PB at 120-150 deg/s and leads by 20.7 +/- 11.7 deg in the EB at 150-180 deg/s.
- Vafidis et al. 2022 eLife 11:e69841 learned path integration: 60 HD + 60 HR units (30 L / 30 R); local delta rule dW_pre,post = eta [f(V_post^axon-proximal) - f(p V_post^axon-distal)] P_pre; after ~20 h simulated (~4,000 cycles) integration gain reaches 1 for |v| < 500 deg/s; diffusion coefficient D = 24.5 deg^2/s (82.3 +/- 15.7 with weight noise sigma ~ 1.5); most 60-s errors within 60 deg; relearning a new gain (0.25-2.0) takes ~3 h. Caveats stated by the authors: no Dale's law, discrete 60-unit ring lets bumps hop, performance 'vastly exceeds' the real fly, adult plasticity undemonstrated.
- LRU parameterisation to copy (Orvieto et al. ICML 2023): lambda_j = exp(-exp(nu_j^log) + i exp(theta_j^log)); input normaliser gamma_j = (1 - |lambda_j|^2)^(1/2). RoPE frequency ladder (Su et al. 2021): theta_i = 10000^(-2i/d) over d/2 two-dimensional subspaces.
- Repository's own null results that constrain the next design (RESULTS.md, all 3 seeds, effects in units of the larger seed SD): connectome recurrent mask vs degree-preserving shuffle = -0.6 / -0.2 / -0.4 SD at N = 256/512/1024 (null at all three); real PN->KC vs shuffled on MQAR = -0.1 SD (null); ring_connectome vs an unstructured RNN of equal size on compass tracking at T = 128 = -9.1 SD (the ring LOST, 0.244 vs 0.669; at T = 512 it was 0.155 vs 0.303 against chance 0.125); mushroom-body memory 0.981 vs Gated DeltaNet 0.998 (-5.6 SD); error gate vs learned gate -2.6 SD (learned gate WON); FlyAI K=3 vs K=0 free-energy steps +0.026 bits/char = +0.7 seed-SD (below the pre-registered 2 SD bar) and -0.6 SD vs a wider control; adaptive-K never fired (K_mean = 3.000 in every run).
- Compute context: everything so far ran on 4 CPU cores with ~135k-parameter models, 400-600 optimiser steps, sequences 32-512 tokens, character-level or synthetic data. The GPU plan (20M-125M hybrids vs a Gated-DeltaNet twin) has not run.

### Dead ends

- THE CONNECTOME GRAPH ITSELF, AS A WEIGHT MASK, IS DEAD. This repository's own H5 result: the real whole-graph mask is indistinguishable from a degree-preserving shuffle at N = 256/512/1024 (-0.6, -0.2, -0.4 seed-SD, CIs spanning 0) and from Erdos-Renyi, while a dense layer of the same N beats it everywhere. H1 found the same for real vs shuffled PN->KC wiring (-0.1 seed-SD). Do not spend another cycle on 'wiring as prior'. The information is in the OFFSETS and the KERNEL SHAPE, which are 3-4 numbers, not in the adjacency matrix.
- SIMULATING THE RING AS A RATE NETWORK, ONE EULER STEP PER TOKEN, IS DEAD. H2: the 148-neuron connectome ring scored 0.244 at T = 128 against 0.669 for an unstructured layer of equal size (-9.1 seed-SD) and stayed near chance at T = 512. Diagnosis from flybrain/modules/ring_attractor.py: one forward Euler step per token with tau = sigmoid(log_tau) and a tanh-limited velocity drive cannot rotate a bump by a meaningful angle per token; the fly runs this circuit for hundreds of membrane time constants per unit of heading change. The fix is to implement the ROTATION (a complex multiply) rather than the substrate. Any Phase-3 design that simulates rate dynamics per token will reproduce this failure.
- NORMALISED-AMPLITUDE RING ATTRACTORS CANNOT CARRY CERTAINTY. Classical Amari/Zhang ring attractors fix bump width and height by the recurrent kernel, independently of input strength; certainty must live in the amplitude, which requires an amplitude-FREE attractor (Kutschireiter et al. 2023). Bolting a scalar 'precision' head onto a normalised ring — which is effectively what the Phase-2 FlyAI model did (amplitude of a bank of 8-unit rings used as precision, but with tanh-bounded dynamics) — does not reproduce the Bayesian semantics and did not help (AIF-H1 null, +0.7 seed-SD).
- SPARSE MUSHROOM-BODY MEMORY DOES NOT BEAT MODERN LINEAR ATTENTION AT MATCHED COMPUTE. H1: MB memory 0.981 vs Gated DeltaNet 0.998, DeltaNet 1.000, linear attention 1.000 on MQAR (-5.6 to -6.3 seed-SD). The error-driven dopamine gate LOST to a learned gate (-2.6 seed-SD) and the delta/depression write did not beat plain Hebb. Whatever the CX design uses for associative memory, it should be a modern linear-attention state, not a re-derived MB.
- POSITING RING ATTRACTORS IN THE FAN-SHAPED BODY IS ANATOMICALLY REJECTED. Reviewers of Sun, Yue & Mangan 2020 (eLife 9:e54026), including S. Heinze, objected that 'there are no known ring attractors in the FB', that the proposed EB->FB direct connections and POTu compass pathway lack support, and that several cell identities were mislabelled (CPU3 vs CPU4, CL2 vs CL1). The FB does vector ARITHMETIC on phasors supplied by the PB; it is not itself an attractor. Build the attractor once, in the compass, and treat the FB as a stateless (or slowly-decaying) ALU plus register file.
- RUNNING THE RAW CONNECTOME AS SPIKING NEURONS IN A BODY DOES NOT PRODUCE CX FUNCTION. The 'Fly.exe' / FruitFly-repo style efforts (2026) zero photoreceptor edges, hand-engineer mappings and are self-declared unvalidated; structure alone does not yield vector computation. Phase offsets, amplitude channels and the rectifying nonlinearity have to be built in explicitly. (Noted in the repo's own LITERATURE.md; treat as moderate-strength secondhand evidence.)
- PERPLEXITY ON CHARACTER-LEVEL TEXT AT ~135k PARAMETERS IS NOT A REGIME WHERE ANY OF THIS CAN WIN. Phase 2's best result was +0.026 bits/char (0.7 seed-SD) and it lost to a merely wider control. Do not pre-register a perplexity claim as the primary metric; use state tracking and length extrapolation, where there are PROOFS that the mechanism is necessary (Merrill et al. 2024; Grazzi et al. 2024).
- THE NAIVE 'INPUT-DEPENDENT ROTARY' IDEA IS LIKELY ALREADY TAKEN. Selective RoPE (arXiv:2511.17388, reported ICLR 2026) is described as an input-dependent learnable rotary embedding generalising RoPE to arbitrary angles, composing with decay gates, implemented as complex gated linear attention, with gains on copying, state tracking and retrieval; and CoPE (arXiv:2405.18719) already makes position a context-dependent cumulative sum of gates. A Phase-3 claim of 'we invented data-dependent rotation' would be false. The defensible novelty is elsewhere: (a) a SINGLE SHARED phase per head rotating all registers (rank-1 phase sharing, from the exp(i phi) factorisation), (b) amplitude as a calibrated Bayesian precision with the closed-form decay, (c) a fixed non-learned offset table as the binding structure, and (d) online inhibitory Hebbian ANCHORING of the phase to context at test time. These must each be ablated separately.
- EXPECTED-FREE-ENERGY / EPISTEMIC-VALUE DECODING HAS NO CX CORRELATE. The CX literature contains no identified epistemic-value signal; exploration in CX models is implemented by LAL oscillators and forward-speed gains, and Dan et al. 2024's goal learning is purely exploitative reinforcement. Carrying the EFE decoder forward into Phase 3 would be importing an axiom the biology does not supply — precisely the error the pivot is meant to correct.
- SUB-COLUMN PHASE OFFSETS CANNOT BE READ FROM THIS MIRROR'S EDGE LIST ALONE. The garyb9/fly-drone mirror of MaleCNS has no per-synapse ROI, so EPG->PEN contacts in the PB (input) and the EB (axo-axonal, at the shifted location) cannot be separated — LIMITATIONS.md already records this. My column-label method recovers offsets only to 45-deg granularity, which is why PFL3 comes out at 87.8 deg rather than 67.5 deg. Getting 67.5 deg requires synapse coordinates within FB columns, i.e. a different data product.

### Open questions

- Does the exp(i phi) factorisation survive when phi is INFERRED from text rather than supplied by a compass? In the fly the heading is anchored to the world; in a language model the 'frame' is latent. What objective makes a single shared phase per head learn something worth sharing, and does it collapse to a constant (the failure mode of every latent-variable LM)?
- Is a SHARED phase (rank-1 across registers) better or worse than per-channel angles at matched parameters? Selective RoPE and RoPE both use per-channel angles. The fly's constraint is strictly stronger. It could be a useful inductive bias or a capacity bottleneck; this is the single most important ablation and it is cheap to run.
- How does a downstream circuit actually READ bump amplitude as certainty? Only the GOAL bump (FC2) has a demonstrated amplitude readout, via PFL3 steering gain (Mussells Pires 2024). Whether steering scales multiplicatively with BOTH the heading amplitude and the goal amplitude has not been measured. If it does not, the 'precision-weighted comparator' term e = |z_s||z_g| sin(dtheta) is partly our invention.
- What is the actual capacity of a bank of phasor registers for in-context binding? Residue hyperdimensional computing gives logarithmic scaling in dynamic range (Kymn et al. 2025) and resonator networks can factor superposed products (Frady et al. 2020), but nobody has measured recall capacity for a phasor register file under the ADDITIVE, non-negative-amplitude constraint the fly obeys.
- Can the ER->EPG-style inhibitory anchoring rule be made to work at test time in a language model without a supervisory signal? In the fly the teacher is self-motion (the fly rotates and sees the scene rotate). The LM analogue of 'self-motion' is unclear — possibly the model's own committed prediction, possibly positional displacement. Getting this wrong is the most likely way the design fails.
- How do several FB registers compete or sum? Multi-goal arbitration is unmeasured: FC2, the 13 hDelta types and the 9 FB layers all hold vectors, and how tangential neurons gate them quantitatively is unknown. Every published model assumes a single static goal. The normalization-not-selection claim (Nanni & Lee 2026) is a preprint I could not verify against the primary text.
- Which CX operations are linear and which are nonlinear? There is no catalogue. Vector inversion needs calcium spikes (Ishida et al. 2025); PFL3's comparison needs a nonlinearity whose exact form (quadratic? rectified?) differs between the Westeinde and Mussells Pires models. A full 'instruction set' of the fan-shaped body does not exist, and the ML design needs one.
- Are the Delta7 kernel and the PFN/PFL offsets the same in MaleCNS v1.0 (male, whole CNS) as in hemibrain and FlyWire (female)? My measurement is from one male animal. No sexual-dimorphism analysis has singled out the CX. The 45-deg quantisation also prevents a clean comparison.
- Is there a defensible SOTA regime at all? Selective RoPE (if real and reproducible) may already occupy the 'rotation + decay' territory on state tracking and retrieval. The Phase-3 pre-registration must first REPRODUCE the strongest prior art, then show the fly-specific constraints add something on top. If they do not, that is a publishable negative and the project should say so.
- Does the FB actually support more than one spatial harmonic? Everything measured is first-harmonic. If the FB is strictly single-harmonic, the register algebra is 2-D per register and cannot bind more than one value per register — which caps in-context capacity and argues for many small registers rather than few large ones. If higher harmonics exist (e.g. in hDelta or vDelta populations), the algebra is richer. Unmeasured.
- What is the right per-token angular increment distribution? The fly's is set by physics (body rotation rates, 145 +/- 82 deg/s bandwidth). A language model has no such prior. Initialising the increment head so phases neither saturate nor freeze is an unsolved practical problem and is exactly where LRU-style initialisation theory (arXiv:2303.06349) should be borrowed rather than reinvented.

### Relevance to language models

"The central complex is the one place in any connectome where a brain has been caught doing explicit, legible linear algebra, and the algebra it does is the same algebra that modern sequence models already run — which is both the opportunity and the trap. The opportunity: the fly's primitives map one-to-one onto machinery that is known to work and is cheap. A phasor population is a complex register; the Delta7 kernel is a rank-2-plus-rank-1 recurrent operator (I measured it at 98.7% first harmonic, W(dphi) = -902 + 944 cos(dphi)); PEN velocity integration is a complex multiply, i.e. exactly RoPE with a data-dependent angle; PFN gain fields are a scalar times a rotation of a value vector; hDelta summation of phase-shifted copies is superposition in a Fourier holographic reduced representation; and PFL3's left-minus-right readout is Im(conj(z_state) z_goal) — a complex inner product, which is an attention logit. The whole steering circuit is therefore a two-term attention head whose query and key are the same registers read at fixed anatomical shifts, with zero learned projections. The trap: because the algebra is the same, most of it is already in the literature. RoPE is a fractional-power phasor code; the LRU's lambda = exp(-exp(nu) + i exp(theta)) with gamma = sqrt(1-|lambda|^2) is a bank of leaky ring attractors with amplitude normalisation; CoPE already integrates gated increments into a context-dependent position; Selective RoPE reportedly already does input-dependent rotation with decay in complex gated linear attention. So the fly cannot contribute 'use rotations'. What it can contribute are four specific CONSTRAINTS that no ML paper imposes: one shared phase per head rotating every register (the exp(i phi) factorisation), amplitude as a calibrated von Mises precision with a closed-form decay rather than a learned gate, a FIXED non-learned offset table (0, +/-45, 180 degrees) as the binding structure with non-negative amplitudes, and an online inhibitory Hebbian rule that anchors the phase to arbitrary context features at test time. Those are testable, ablatable, and they live in a regime — state tracking, modular counting, permutation composition, rebinding, length extrapolation — where rotations are provably necessary and where SSMs and positive-eigenvalue linear RNNs provably fail. That is the only honest place to aim a state-of-the-art claim."


## Sensory coding in the fly: normalisation, expansion, sparsification, temporal differentiation and efficient coding — the mathematics, the numbers, and what an engineer can actually reuse

*Key: `sensory-coding`.*

### Summary

The fly's sensory front end is best read as four theorems with numbers attached, not as a wiring diagram. (1) NORMALISATION. The antennal lobe divides each channel by a pooled measure of total population activity: R_i = R_max s_i^1.5 / (s_i^1.5 + sigma^1.5 + m^1.5), m proportional to summed ORN drive, implemented by presynaptic GABA_B inhibition; it compresses a 57x concentration range to 6.6x and equalises inter-odour distances. The same equation, at other parameter settings, is softmax, RMSNorm, whitening, contrast invariance, marginalisation over a nuisance variable, and attention - so the transferable content is not the formula but the placement: normalise before every thresholded projection, and again as scalar feedback after it. (2) EXPANSION. 50 channels to 2,000 Kenyon cells with fan-in 5-7. Cover's theorem says the linear part adds nothing; the threshold does all the work, taking random-dichotomy capacity from ~100 to ~4,000. Fan-in has an interior optimum (too small = no mixing, too large = shared inputs, correlation ~ K/N) that maximises the participation ratio (tr C)^2/tr(C^2) at K ~ 7 for the fly and K ~ 4 for cerebellar granule cells - both matching anatomy. (3) SPARSIFICATION. One GABAergic neuron (APL, contacting ~100 % of KCs) holds the active fraction near 5 %; removing it doubles activity, re-correlates odours and selectively destroys fine discrimination. The optimal coding level is interior and set by input SNR and task smoothness, not "as sparse as possible". Sparseness is what permits one-shot local writes (0.69 bits/synapse at optimal sparseness; capacity ~ 1/(f ln 1/f)) and is forced by 2-6x10^4 ATP per bit. (4) TIME. The front end transmits derivatives (ORN spiking is a differentiating filter; PNs signal rate plus rate-of-change) with a ladder of adaptation timescales (250 ms, seconds, 10 s), and - the deepest result - rapid gain rescaling is made unambiguous by transmitting the slow gain variable itself as a signal. Vision adds one more: direction selectivity is a bilinear operator between two DIFFERENTLY filtered arms with an offset suppressive term, and the multiplication is a real shunting conductance. That asymmetric-arm bilinear operator is the least-explored, most mathematically specified idea available here.

### Established (with sources)

- Antennal-lobe gain control is divisive normalisation by TOTAL population activity, not by identity: PN firing rate follows R_i = R_max * s_i^n / (s_i^n + sigma^n + m^n) with n ~ 1.5 and m proportional to the summed ORN activity across all glomeruli (Olsen, Bhandawat & Wilson 2010, Neuron 66:287-299). Private-odour (no-lateral-input) fits in that paper give R_max = 170/167/163/144 and sigma = 16.3/11.8/12.4/44.8 for glomeruli DM4/DL5/VM7/DM1.
- The mechanism of that normalisation is lateral PRESYNAPTIC inhibition onto ORN terminals (GABA_B, with a GABA_A component), whose strength scales with total feedforward drive to the whole antennal lobe and is similarly tuned in different glomeruli (Olsen & Wilson 2008, Nature 452:956-960).
- The antennal lobe also amplifies weak inputs and compresses strong ones, which broadens PN tuning, equalises distances between odour representations and increases trial-to-trial reliability relative to ORNs (Bhandawat, Olsen, Gouwens, Schlief & Wilson 2007, Nature Neuroscience 10:1474-1482).
- The olfactory pathway normalises TWICE: once in the antennal lobe (LN-mediated presynaptic inhibition) and again at the expansion layer via a single wide-field GABAergic neuron (APL in Drosophila, GGN in locust) that implements the normalisation denominator with one cell (Papadopoulou, Cassenaer, Nowotny & Laurent 2011, Science 332:721-725; Lin, Bygrave, de Calignon, Lee & Miesenboeck 2014, Nature Neuroscience 17:559-568).
- PN -> Kenyon-cell connectivity is, to first order, random: each KC integrates a different, apparently random combination of glomeruli (Caron, Ruta, Abbott & Axel 2013, Nature 497:113-117). Later EM work shows statistically detectable structure on top of that randomness: food-responsive PNs converge preferentially onto particular KC subtypes, partly because KCs of different neuroblast origin can only reach certain boutons (Zheng et al. 2022, Current Biology, 'Structured sampling of olfactory input by the fly mushroom body').
- The expansion layer sparsifies hard: the fraction of cells responding to an odour drops from 59 % (PNs) to 6 % (KCs), with three contributing causes - fast EPSP decay limiting temporal integration, low PN convergence per KC, and high KC spike threshold (Turner, Bazhenov & Laurent 2008, J Neurophysiol 99:734-746).
- Sparseness is actively maintained by KC->APL->KC negative feedback: blocking the loop roughly doubles the active fraction, raises inter-odour correlations, and selectively impairs learned discrimination of SIMILAR (not dissimilar) odours (Lin et al. 2014, Nature Neuroscience 17:559-568). Sparsity is therefore a discriminability device, not an efficiency ornament.
- The dimension (participation ratio) of a random expansion has an interior maximum in the per-neuron in-degree K, and the maximising K matches anatomy: K ~ 6-7 for Drosophila KCs (~50 glomerular channels, ~2,000 KCs) and K ~ 4 for cerebellar granule cells (Litwin-Kumar, Harris, Axel, Sompolinsky & Abbott 2017, Neuron 93:1153-1164). Small K fails to mix; large K makes expansion neurons share inputs, so pairwise correlation grows ~K/N and dimension collapses. Global inhibition raises the optimal K by removing the common mode.
- Sparse (not dense) connectivity is what enables decorrelation and pattern separation when inputs are spatially correlated, in a matched cerebellar model (Cayco-Gajic, Clopath & Silver 2017, Nature Communications 8:1116).
- There is an interior optimum in the CODING LEVEL (fraction active): random expansion amplifies input noise, and the amplification grows as the code gets sparser, while the decorrelation benefit grows only logarithmically (Babadi & Sompolinsky 2014, Neuron 83:1213-1226). The optimal coding level is additionally task-dependent - below 0.1 for random categorisation but substantially denser for smooth, low-dimensional target functions (Xie, Muscinelli, Harris & Litwin-Kumar 2023, eLife 12:e82914).
- Linear expansion alone provably buys nothing: a linear map cannot increase the number of linearly separable dichotomies. All the benefit comes from the threshold/k-WTA nonlinearity applied to the expanded code (Cover 1965, IEEE Trans Electron Comput EC-14:326-334; restated for this circuit in the C1 case study of stootoon/circuits-to-algorithms).
- The expansion + k-WTA motif is a locality-sensitive hash: expected tag overlap is monotone increasing in input correlation, and the fly variant (sparse binary projection, expansion rather than reduction, WTA rather than sign bits) beats classical LSH on real datasets, most strongly at short code lengths (Dasgupta, Stevens & Navlakha 2017, Science 358:793-796; the underlying SimHash identity E[sign(w.x)sign(w.y)] = 1 - (2/pi) arccos(cos theta) is Goemans-Williamson 1995 / Charikar 2002).
- The fly's olfactory front end transmits DERIVATIVES, not values: ORN transduction is a ligand-receptor kinetic stage with adaptive feedback, followed by a spiking stage that is a differentiating (high-pass) linear filter, stereotyped across odours and cells (Nagel & Wilson 2011, Nature Neuroscience 14:208-216). Downstream, PN output signals the OSN rate AND its rate of change (Kim, Lazar & Slutskiy 2015, eLife 4:e06651).
- Adaptation in this pathway is multi-timescale and mechanistically layered: front-end Weber-Fechner gain control at tau ~ 250 ms (Kadakia & Emonet 2019, eLife 8:e45293), fast divisive normalisation in the AL, and a slow presynaptic-depression stage with tau ~ 10 s that rescales PN gain to ~50 % without changing the population identity vector and is unaffected by picrotoxin or GABA_B manipulation (Martelli & Fiala 2019, eLife 8:e43735).
- Front-end adaptation and population normalisation are complementary and additive, not redundant: with both, odour-identity classification across four decades of concentration is ~95 % / ~85 % (100 / 1000 odour identities) versus ~70 % / ~40 % with neither (Kadakia & Emonet 2019, eLife 8:e45293).
- Adaptive rescaling in the fly visual system is information-optimal and fast: H1 rescales its input-output function to the stimulus standard deviation within tens of milliseconds, spanning timescales from tens of ms to minutes; the rapid component maximises information about fast variations within the current ensemble while the overall firing rate simultaneously encodes WHICH ensemble the system is in, resolving the resulting ambiguity (Brenner, Bialek & de Ruyter van Steveninck 2000, Neuron 26:695-702; Fairhall, Lewen, Bialek & de Ruyter van Steveninck 2001, Nature 412:787-792).
- Efficient coding was first validated in the fly: the contrast-response function of blowfly large monopolar cells matches the cumulative distribution of contrasts in the natural habitat, i.e. g(s) = y_max * CDF(s), the infomax solution for a bounded scalar channel - two independent measurements, one parameter-free prediction (Laughlin 1981, Z Naturforsch 36c:910-912).
- Predictive coding in its original, non-Friston sense was also a fly/retina result: lateral inhibition implements the optimal linear predictor of a neighbour's signal, and the predictor's strength must weaken as SNR falls - which is observed (Srinivasan, Laughlin & Dubs 1982, Proc R Soc B 216:427-459; generalised by Atick & Redlich 1990/1992, Neural Computation, giving whitening at high SNR, low-pass at low SNR, hence band-pass centre-surround filters).
- Divisive normalisation is the redundancy-reducing transform for a Gaussian-scale-mixture world: R_i = L_i^2 / (sigma^2 + sum_j w_ij L_j^2) removes the hidden local-contrast variable that linear decorrelation cannot touch, with weights predicted from natural-image statistics and then verified against V1 surround suppression without refitting (Schwartz & Simoncelli 2001, Nature Neuroscience 4:819-825; Carandini & Heeger 2012, Nature Reviews Neuroscience 13:51-62).
- The lateral horn is the fly's second, parallel readout of the SAME PN code, and it has opposite statistics to the mushroom body: LH neurons are stereotyped across animals (KCs are not), respond to ~3x more odours than PNs but at lower rates, and carry odour-CATEGORY information that uniglomerular PNs do not (Frechter et al. 2019, eLife: 410 cells recorded, 242 LHONs; Jeanne, Fisek & Wilson 2018, Neuron: 39 LHN types, over-represented convergence of ecologically related glomerulus pairs, stereotyped PN->LHN connectivity).
- LH 'innate valence' is real but partial and not a clean labelled line: a 2,444-line split-GAL4 screen yielded 82 accessible LH cell types, and optogenetic activation of 89 lines produced a valence phenotype in only ~15 % of lines (12 aversive, 3 approach); ~30 % of LH output neurons overlap the mushroom body's DAN dendrites or MBON axons, so innate and learned channels are already mixed in the fly (Dolan et al. 2019, eLife 8:e43079).
- Direction selectivity in T4/T5 is NOT a pure Hassenstein-Reichardt correlator nor a pure Barlow-Levick detector: both mechanisms operate on opposite sides of the same dendrite - preferred-direction enhancement plus null-direction suppression - giving high direction selectivity at the first stage where direction exists (Haag, Arenz, Serbe, Gabbiani & Borst 2016, eLife 5:e17421; Leong, Esch, Poole, Reiser & Clandinin 2016, J Neurosci 36:8078-8092; reviewed in Borst, Haag & Mauss 2020, J Comp Physiol A 206:109-124).
- The temporal tuning of the motion detector is inherited from the temporal filters of its input neurons: cells synapsing on the preferred and null sides of the dendrite are low-pass (Mi4, Mi9, CT1 / Tm9, CT1), while those on the central arm are band-pass (Mi1, Tm3 / Tm1, Tm2) (Arenz, Drews, Richter, Ammer & Borst 2017, Current Biology 27:929-944). A passive conductance model of T4 with fast excitation plus spatially offset, delayed inhibition reproduces the responses (Gruntman, Romani & Reiser 2018, Nature Neuroscience).
- Multiplication by a single neuron is biophysically real in this circuit, implemented by voltage-dependent/shunting conductance interaction on the T4 dendrite (Groschner, Malis, Zuidinga & Borst 2022, Nature 603:118-123, 'A biophysical account of multiplication by a single neuron'). Gating is not a machine-learning convenience; it is what the dendrite does.
- A correlation-type motion detector is tuned to TEMPORAL FREQUENCY, not velocity: the behavioural and cellular optimum sits near ~1 Hz independent of pattern wavelength, so speed and spatial scale are confounded by construction (classical Reichardt-model result; see Borst, Haag & Mauss 2020 and the Egelhaaf/Borst literature).
- Sparse codes are what make one-shot, local, gradient-free writes possible: a sparse index set lets a downstream readout be modified without credit assignment, which is why the mushroom body can learn an association in a single trial, and why the same code supports a one-weight-per-KC Bloom-filter novelty detector (Dasgupta, Sheehan, Stevens & Navlakha 2018, PNAS 115:13093-13098).
- Classical capacity theory says the same thing quantitatively: binary-synapse associative memories reach ln 2 ~ 0.69 bits per synapse only at extreme sparseness (Willshaw, Buneman & Longuet-Higgins 1969, Nature 222:960-962), and capacity in units of patterns grows roughly as 1/(f ln(1/f)) as the coding level f falls (Tsodyks & Feigel'man 1988, Europhys Lett 6:101-105).
- Information in this system is metabolically expensive, which is why the code is sparse: ~2-6 x 10^4 ATP per bit at a chemical synapse, ~10^6 ATP per bit for a graded interneuron signal, ~7 x 10^6 for a photoreceptor (Laughlin, de Ruyter van Steveninck & Anderson 1998, Nature Neuroscience 1:36-41). Sparseness is the solution to an energy-per-bit constraint, and 'as sparse as possible' is not the optimum - cost per spike trades against bits per spike.

### Principles and their translation

| principle | biology / mathematics | translation to a sequence model | evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| Normalise before you threshold; normalise twice. | A thresholded random projection is only well defined on a scale-controlled input. The fly normalises at the antennal lobe (presynaptic GABA_B inhibition scaled by TOTAL ORN drive; Olsen & Wilson 2008; Olsen et al. 2010) and again at the expansion layer (APL/GGN, a single neuron computing the denominator; Papadopoulou et al. 2011; Lin et al. 2014). Divisive form: r_i = g * x_i^n / (sigma^n + sum_j w_ij x_j^n). | Any sparse-expansion / top-k memory layer in an LM must be preceded by an explicit normalisation of the key vector AND followed by a global feedback normalisation of the selected units. Concretely: RMSNorm the input to the memory layer, then apply a learned global inhibition on the top-k logits (subtract a scalar function of the summed pre-activation) before selecting. This is a one-line change that the fly says is not optional; the dead-latent pathology of top-k SAEs (up to 90 % dead latents without mitigation, Gao et al. 2024, arXiv:2406.04093) is exactly the failure this feedback prevents. | established |
| One normalisation equation, six algorithmic readings - choose the one you need and fix its parameters from the reading, not by fitting. | r_i = g x_i^n / (sigma^n + sum_j w_ij x_j^n). sigma -> 0 gives scale invariance; n -> infinity gives winner-take-all; finite n on log inputs is softmax at inverse temperature n; n = 2, sigma = 0, structured w is whitening/redundancy reduction for Gaussian scale mixtures; quadratic pooling plus division is approximate Bayesian marginalisation of a nuisance variable (concentration); multiplicative modulation before normalisation is attention (Carandini & Heeger 2012; Schwartz & Simoncelli 2001). | Softmax attention, RMSNorm and gating are the SAME operator at different parameter settings. A design that claims novelty here must state which reading it uses and predict a parameter it did not fit. The unexplained biological fact is the exponent n ~ 1.5 rather than 2 - an empirical knob worth sweeping in an LM normalisation layer (n as a learned scalar per layer), since nothing in ML has tested non-quadratic pooling exponents systematically. | promising |
| Expansion is worthless without the nonlinearity; the nonlinearity is worthless without the sparse fan-in. | Cover (1965): a linear map cannot increase the number of linearly separable dichotomies. Separable dichotomies C(P,M) = 2 * sum_{k=0}^{M-1} binom(P-1,k), so random-dichotomy capacity rises from ~2*50 = 100 patterns at the PN layer to ~2*2000 = 4000 at the KC layer - but only because of the threshold. Fan-in must be small or expansion units share inputs and re-correlate (pairwise correlation ~ K/N). | Build the memory/expansion layer as h = TopK(A * x_norm) with A SPARSE (a fixed number s of non-zeros per row) rather than dense. Sparse binary projections are ~20x cheaper than dense Gaussian ones at equal quality in the LSH setting (Dasgupta et al. 2017). For an LM this is the difference between an expansion layer that costs O(M*d) and one that costs O(M*s). | established |
| There is an interior optimum in fan-in K, and it is measurable with a cheap, differentiable objective. | Dimension = participation ratio PR = (sum_i lambda_i)^2 / sum_i lambda_i^2 = (tr C)^2 / tr(C^2) of the expansion-layer covariance C. PR is maximised at K ~ 6-7 for the fly (N ~ 50 channels, M ~ 2000 KCs, measured 5-7 claws) and K ~ 4 for cerebellar granule cells (measured ~4) (Litwin-Kumar et al. 2017). Global inhibition raises the optimum by removing the common mode. | Do not copy K = 7. Measure it: sweep the per-latent fan-in s of the sparse projection and pick the s that maximises PR of the expansion-layer activations on real token data, then check whether PR ranking predicts downstream loss. If PR-optimal s also minimises loss, that is a genuine, cheap, transferable design rule and a publishable result; if it does not, that is a clean negative that kills a widely repeated claim. The common extrapolation 'K_opt ~ sqrt(N_in)' (giving K ~ 15-30 for a 512-2048-d stage) is NOT established by the paper and must be treated as a hypothesis. | promising |
| The coding level (fraction active) has an interior optimum set by input SNR and by the smoothness of the target function - it is not 'as sparse as possible'. | Random expansion amplifies input noise, and amplification grows with sparseness (relative corruption ~ epsilon * sqrt(2 ln(1/alpha))), while decorrelation gain grows only logarithmically; hence an interior optimum in alpha (Babadi & Sompolinsky 2014). Optimal alpha is task-dependent: < 0.1 for random categorisation, substantially denser for smooth low-dimensional targets (Xie et al. 2023, eLife 12:e82914). The fly sits at alpha ~ 0.05-0.10. | k in a top-k memory layer is a first-class hyperparameter governed by the noise level of the incoming representation, not a fixed 'biological' 5 %. Prediction to test: the optimal k should INCREASE with representation noise (e.g. lower precision, earlier layers, noisier data) and DECREASE for tasks that are closer to random associations (factual recall, key-value retrieval) versus smooth tasks (next-token prediction on natural text). Observing alpha measures input SNR. | promising |
| A single global inhibitory unit is enough to implement the normalisation denominator, sparsify, decorrelate and keep units alive. | APL (one GABAergic neuron per hemisphere, contacting essentially 100 % of KCs) and locust GGN implement 'one-neuron normalisation'. Removing it roughly doubles the active fraction, raises inter-odour correlations and impairs discrimination of similar odours only (Lin et al. 2014; Papadopoulou et al. 2011). | Replace per-latent auxiliary losses and resampling heuristics with one scalar feedback unit: i = w^T relu(h), h <- relu(a - i), iterated 1-3 times, or equivalently a soft k-WTA with an adaptive scalar threshold. This is cheaper than auxiliary-loss machinery and is the mechanism biology actually uses. Testable claim: scalar-feedback k-WTA reduces dead latents and improves reconstruction/loss at matched FLOPs versus TopK + auxiliary loss. | promising |
| Sensory front ends transmit derivatives and normalised deviations, not raw values. | ORN spiking stage is a differentiating linear filter stereotyped across odours and cells (Nagel & Wilson 2011); PNs signal the OSN rate plus its rate of change (Kim, Lazar & Slutskiy 2015); retina/LMC lateral inhibition is the optimal linear predictor, so what is transmitted is the prediction ERROR (Srinivasan, Laughlin & Dubs 1982). At high SNR the optimal filter whitens (/K(f)/^2 ~ 1/S(f)); at low SNR it low-passes; the combination is band-pass. | For streaming / long-context models, feed the sequence mixer both the current normalised state and its temporal difference with several fixed timescales, and let the model spend capacity on the residual rather than on re-encoding the predictable part. Concretely: per-channel exponential moving averages at a geometric ladder of time constants with the deviation (x - EMA_tau(x)) as the actual input. This is a cheap, principled alternative to learned gates and is the one place where the biology gives an explicit FILTER SHAPE, not just an architecture. | promising |
| Adaptation is multi-timescale, and the slow variable itself carries information about which regime you are in. | Weber-Fechner front-end gain control tau ~ 250 ms, fast divisive normalisation, slow presynaptic depression tau ~ 10 s that rescales gain to ~50 % without changing the identity vector (Kadakia & Emonet 2019; Martelli & Fiala 2019). H1 rescales to stimulus SD in tens of ms; rapid adaptation maximises information within the local ensemble while the firing rate encodes the ensemble identity, resolving the ambiguity adaptation would otherwise create (Brenner et al. 2000; Fairhall et al. 2001). | A model that renormalises its activations to running statistics destroys absolute-scale information unless the normaliser's own state is also read out. Design rule: whenever you divide by a running statistic, feed log(statistic) forward as an explicit feature. For non-stationary text streams (topic switches, document boundaries) this gives the model an explicit, cheap 'which distribution am I in' channel, and it is the correct fix for the naive arousal/surprise unit that failed in phase 2 - the failure there was using surprise as a GAIN, not as a FEATURE. | promising |
| The transfer function should be the CDF of the input distribution; neuron/parameter density should follow the prior. | g(s) = y_max * CDF(s) maximises information for a bounded scalar channel with fixed output noise (Laughlin 1981, verified in blowfly LMC against natural contrast statistics with no free parameters). Population version: with Fisher information J(s) ~ c * T * g(s) * d(s)^2, infomax gives neuron density d(s) proportional to p(s) and warping F(s) = CDF (Ganguli & Simoncelli 2014, Neural Computation 26:2103-2134); discrimination threshold ~ 1/p(s). | Quantile/CDF transforms of continuous features, and allocation of vocabulary or memory slots proportional to usage probability, are the same theorem. For an LM memory layer this predicts that key-space should be allocated by frequency (a learned quantile warp on the routing logits), and it gives a principled objection to uniform product-key grids. | established |
| Two parallel readouts of one normalised code: a stereotyped low-dimensional categorical pathway and a random high-dimensional plastic pathway. | The same ~50 PN channels feed the lateral horn (stereotyped across animals, categorical, innate, compact - 39 LHN types in Jeanne et al. 2018; category information absent in PNs but present in LHONs, Frechter et al. 2019) and the mushroom body (random, 40x expansion, individually unique, one-trial plastic). Extreme mixed selectivity maximises the number of decodable dichotomies but costs generalisation to novel stimuli; the stereotyped pathway supplies the generalisation. | Split the head: one dense, smooth, slowly-learned pathway for generalisation, and one sparse, high-dimensional, fast-writable pathway for memorisation, both reading the SAME normalised representation, combined by a learned convex mixture. This is the fly's actual answer to the generalisation/memorisation trade-off, and it maps onto (dense transformer block) + (sparse memory layer) - a combination that already wins at scale: memory-layer models outperform dense models with >2x the compute and MoE at matched compute and parameters, with the gains concentrated on FACTUAL tasks (Berges et al. 2024, arXiv:2412.09764). | promising |
| Motion detection = asymmetric temporal filtering + multiplicative interaction + opponent subtraction, with BOTH enhancement and suppression. | Hassenstein-Reichardt: R = f(x1(t)) * g(x2(t)) - f(x2(t)) * g(x1(t)) where f and g are differently filtered (one delayed); Barlow-Levick: delayed inhibition vetoes the null direction. T4/T5 do both on opposite sides of one dendrite (Haag et al. 2016; Leong et al. 2016), with low-pass filters on the flanking arms and band-pass in the centre (Arenz et al. 2017), and the multiplication is a real shunting/voltage-dependent conductance operation (Groschner et al. 2022, Nature 603:118-123). | This is a bilinear (second-order Volterra) operator with per-channel timescales and a signed veto - i.e. exactly the algebra of gated linear attention / state-space models, but with two design details ML usually omits: (i) the two multiplied streams should have DIFFERENT, fixed temporal filters (one low-pass, one band-pass), not the same projection; (ii) the negative arm should be a spatially/temporally OFFSET suppression, not a symmetric subtraction. A gated-linear-attention variant built from a low-pass arm times a band-pass arm minus its mirror is a concrete, novel, testable sequence operator with an exact biological derivation. | speculative |
| Sparse codes exist so that a single, local, gradient-free write is possible - capacity and learnability are the same design constraint. | A sparse index set permits modification of the readout weights of the few active units without credit assignment; binary-synapse capacity reaches ln 2 ~ 0.69 bits/synapse only at extreme sparseness (Willshaw et al. 1969) and pattern capacity scales ~ 1/(f ln(1/f)) (Tsodyks & Feigel'man 1988). The fly exploits this for one-trial learning at KC->MBON synapses and for a one-weight-per-KC novelty/Bloom filter (Dasgupta et al. 2018, PNAS 115:13093-13098). | If test-time learning is the target regime, the write must touch O(k) parameters, not O(d^2). That is the actual argument for a sparse memory layer over a dense fast-weight matrix - not perplexity per FLOP but writes per FLOP and interference per write. The measurable claim is forgetting/interference, not loss: sparse writes should show markedly lower interference than dense fast weights at matched write cost (already supported at scale by sparse-memory finetuning results, arXiv:2510.15103, uncertain provenance). | promising |

### Numbers an engineer can use

- Antennal lobe: divisive-normalisation exponent n = 1.5 (Olsen, Bhandawat & Wilson 2010, Neuron 66:287). Per-glomerulus private-odour fits: R_max = 170 / 167 / 163 / 144 spikes/s and sigma = 16.3 / 11.8 / 12.4 / 44.8 for DM4 / DL5 / VM7 / DM1.
- Commonly used reimplementation parameters for the same equation: R_max = 165, sigma = 10.5, m = 0.05, a = 1.5 in R2_i = R_max * R1_i^a / (sigma^a + R1_i^a + (m * sum_j R1_j)^a). PROVENANCE UNCERTAIN - found in open-source reimplementations (e.g. fly-olfaction simulators on GitHub) rather than verified in the primary text; a 2025 eLife reviewed preprint (Shen et al., 108236) uses sigma = 12, n = 1.5, k = 0.1.
- Concentration compression: total ORN drive spans 57x over four decades of odour concentration; total PN drive is compressed to ~6.6x (Olsen et al. 2010, via secondary summary - the ratio, not the raw fit, is the load-bearing number).
- Drosophila olfactory anatomy: ~50-51 glomerular channels; ~1,357 OSNs across 51 receptor types and ~141 uniglomerular PNs in the hemibrain-derived model (Lazar et al. 2021, eLife 62362); ~150 PNs give olfactory input to the mushroom body, of which 129 uniglomerular olfactory PNs supply 63.6 % of KC input (hemibrain MB connectome).
- Mushroom body size: 1,927 KCs in 14 subtypes (hemibrain, one hemisphere); this project's own MaleCNS extraction measures 2,019 (L) / 2,045 (R) KCs, expansion ratio ~40x over ~50 channels.
- KC fan-in ('claws'): ~5-7 PN channels per KC. This project's MaleCNS extraction: mean 5.30 (L) / 5.23 (R) PN neurons per KC, mean 5.16 / 5.07 PN TYPES, median 5. Turner et al. 2008 estimated ~7 PNs per KC from spontaneous EPSP statistics and ~10 on average physiologically. Litwin-Kumar et al. 2017 use ~6-7 as the anatomical value.
- PN fan-out: mean 82.6 (L) / 83.2 (R) KCs per PN; 9,917 / 9,898 PN->KC edges; 183,709 / 189,838 anatomical PN->KC synaptic contacts (this project's MaleCNS extraction; contacts are anatomical counts, not weights).
- APL: exactly 1 GABAergic neuron per hemisphere, contacting 100 % (L) / 99.95 % (R) of KCs (this project's MaleCNS extraction), consistent with hemibrain.
- Sparsification: fraction of cells responding to an odour falls from 59 % (PNs) to 6 % (KCs) (Turner, Bazhenov & Laurent 2008, J Neurophysiol 99:734-746). Population imaging gives ~5 % of ~2,500 KCs active per odour, largely independent of concentration and chemical complexity (Honegger, Campbell & Turner 2011, J Neurosci 31:11772-11785).
- Blocking APL feedback raises the active KC fraction from ~10 % to ~20 % and raises inter-odour correlations; it impairs learned discrimination of similar but not dissimilar odours (Lin et al. 2014, Nature Neuroscience 17:559-568).
- KC temporal integration window: EPSP 10-90 % rise 2.1 +/- 0.5 ms, single-exponential decay 11.5 +/- 5.3 ms (Turner et al. 2008). Responsive PNs fire ~162 spikes/s during a stimulus while a KC emits 1-2 spikes.
- Optimal in-degree from participation-ratio theory: K_opt ~ 6-7 for Drosophila KCs (N ~ 50, M ~ 2000) and K_opt ~ 4 for cerebellar granule cells; both match anatomy (Litwin-Kumar et al. 2017, Neuron 93:1153-1164). Participation ratio of KC input currents under olfactory connectivity reported as 15.4 (secondary source, uncertain).
- Shared-input cost: expected pairwise correlation between two randomly sampled expansion units ~ K/N (K = fan-in, N = number of input channels). At K = 7, N = 50 this is 0.14 - the fly sits right where mixing is achieved but correlation is still small.
- Coding level: fly alpha ~ 0.05-0.10. Theory: optimal alpha is interior (Babadi & Sompolinsky 2014) and task-dependent - below 0.1 for random categorisation, denser for smooth targets (Xie et al. 2023, eLife 12:e82914). Relative noise corruption in a sparse tag grows as ~ epsilon * sqrt(2 ln(1/alpha)) (secondary source).
- Separable-dichotomy capacity (Cover 1965): ~100 patterns at the 50-dimensional PN layer versus ~4,000 at the 2,000-dimensional KC layer, i.e. 2M for random dichotomies - the entire gain comes from the threshold nonlinearity.
- Sparse associative-memory capacity: ln 2 ~ 0.69 bits per synapse for binary synapses at optimal sparseness (Willshaw et al. 1969); pattern capacity scales ~ 1/(f ln(1/f)) with coding level f (Tsodyks & Feigel'man 1988).
- LSH behaviour of the fly hash: expected tag overlap O(rho) ~ alpha^((1-rho)/(1+rho)), whose exponent is exactly the Indyk-Motwani LSH amplification parameter (derivation from a secondary source, Slepian-inequality argument; treat the closed form as unverified, the monotonicity as solid).
- FlyHash empirical: on MNIST (784-d) at hash length k = 4, mAP 16.0 % (classical LSH) versus 44.8 % (fly); gains largest at short codes; sparse binary projection ~20x cheaper to compute than dense Gaussian (Dasgupta, Stevens & Navlakha 2017, Science 358:793-796; numbers via secondary reimplementation, verify before citing).
- Compressed-sensing bound on the olfactory front end: with M ~ 50 receptor types and N ~ 10^4 possible odorants, l1 recovery (M >= C k log(N/k)) supports k ~ 3 simultaneously identifiable odorants; with M ~ 10^3 receptors (mouse) k ~ 100 - both close to psychophysical mixture-resolution limits.
- Adaptation time constants to reuse as a ladder: ORN Weber-Fechner front end tau ~ 250 ms; AL divisive normalisation effectively instantaneous; slow presynaptic depression tau ~ 10 s (fast component 300-400 ms) adapting PN responses to ~50 % (degree of adaptation 0.57 for PNs versus -0.06 +/- 0.1 for ORNs); PN calcium adaptation timescales 3-40 s (Kadakia & Emonet 2019; Martelli & Fiala 2019).
- Identity classification across four decades of concentration (model with 50 ORN types, 150-d odour space): ~95 % / ~85 % (100 / 1000 identities) with both front-end adaptation and divisive normalisation; ~92 / ~82 with adaptation only; ~88 / ~65 with normalisation only; ~70 / ~40 with neither. Mutual information ~2.5-3 bits versus 0.5-1.5 bits without adaptation (Kadakia & Emonet 2019, eLife 8:e45293).
- Lateral horn: 496 LH cell types in the hemibrain (~1,428 third-order olfactory neurons have LH dendrites); 82 types accessible by 2,444 split-GAL4 lines; 44 LHON types (26 cholinergic, 13 GABAergic, 9 glutamatergic); optogenetic screen of 89 lines gave valence in only ~15 % (12 aversive covering 6 cell types, 3 approach) (Dolan et al. 2019, eLife 8:e43079).
- LH physiology: 410 cells recorded (242 LHONs, 84 LHLNs, 84 PNs); LHONs respond to 35 % of odour-cell pairs versus 12 % for PNs, at 14 Hz versus 21 Hz evoked, with ~10x lower baseline; ~70 % of category-informative cells are LHONs (Frechter et al. 2019, eLife).
- Visual system scale: ~800 ommatidia/columns per eye; ~37,500 intrinsic neurons in the right optic lobe; 4 T4 and 4 T5 subtypes with roughly one cell of each subtype per column (~800 each) (Matsliah et al. 2024, Nature, 'Neuronal parts list and wiring diagram for a visual system').
- Motion-detector tuning: optimum near ~1 Hz TEMPORAL FREQUENCY, independent of pattern wavelength and hence of absolute velocity - the classical signature (and limitation) of a correlation-type detector.
- Energy per bit (the reason for sparseness): 2-6 x 10^4 ATP per bit at a chemical synapse; ~10^6 ATP per bit for a graded interneuron signal; ~7 x 10^6 ATP per bit for a photoreceptor (Laughlin, de Ruyter van Steveninck & Anderson 1998, Nature Neuroscience 1:36-41).
- Scaling law reported for the expansion layer across insects: number of KCs scales as (number of glomeruli)^(7/2) (attributed to Stevens 2015, PNAS 112:9460-9465, via secondary summary - UNCERTAIN, verify in the primary text before using; if true it implies expansion ratio grows with input dimension, which contradicts a fixed 40x rule).
- Modern ML anchors for the same motif, with numbers: memory-layer LMs outperform dense models with >2x compute and MoE at matched compute+parameters, demonstrated to 128B memory parameters and 1T training tokens (Berges et al. 2024, arXiv:2412.09764); top-k sparse autoencoders at LLM scale use k ~ 32 with up to 16M latents and show a power-law between L0 and reconstruction MSE, with up to 90 % dead latents absent mitigations (Gao et al. 2024, arXiv:2406.04093); FlyVec sparse binary embeddings use ~3 % of the memory of continuous embeddings and are competitive at small hash lengths but worse than word2vec in absolute quality (Liang et al. 2021, ICLR, arXiv:2101.06887).

### Dead ends

- The specific PN->KC wiring carries nothing beyond fan-in for non-olfactory tasks. This project's own H1d was null (-0.1 seed-SD) for real versus random wiring at matched fan-in, and that is the EXPECTED result: Caron, Ruta, Abbott & Axel 2013 (Nature 497:113) showed the wiring is random to first order. The measurable structure that does exist (Zheng et al. 2022, Current Biology: food-responsive PNs over-converge onto specific KC subtypes; up to ~7-15x glomerulus-specific LN weight asymmetries in FlyWire) encodes ODOUR ecology, not token statistics. Do not re-run this experiment.
- Hard-coded divisive normalisation as a module competing with learned normalisation. LayerNorm/RMSNorm/softmax are already the sigma, n = 2 members of the same family; and a 2025 J Neurosci result (in this repo's LITERATURE.md) shows explicit divisive-normalisation Bayesian circuits suffer a curse of dimensionality and are outperformed by scalable learned feedforward approximations. The transferable content is WHERE to normalise (before every thresholded projection, and again in feedback after it) and WHAT to feed forward (the normaliser's own state), not the formula itself.
- A fixed random projection at matched parameters versus a learned one. BioHash (Ryali, Hopfield, Grinberg & Krotov 2020, ICML/PMLR v119, arXiv:2001.04907) shows that learning the sparse projection with a local plasticity rule beats the data-independent fly hash. The fly's randomness is a developmental constraint (a 13,000-gene genome cannot specify 10^4 synapses), not an optimum. Any 'random projection is better' claim should be expected to fail; the honest claims are about COST (sparse binary is ~20x cheaper) and about behaviour at very short code lengths.
- Linear expansion layers, or expansion without a matched inhibitory feedback. Cover's theorem says the linear part adds zero separable dichotomies, and the empirical top-k SAE literature shows what happens when the k-WTA lacks the APL analogue: up to 90 % dead latents (Gao et al. 2024). An expansion layer without a normalisation denominator is a known failure mode in both fields.
- Extremely sparse codes for token prediction. This repo's own analysis notes that f ~ 0.001 / one-hot VQ codes are 50-100x sparser than the biological optimum (f ~ 0.05-0.1) and hit an entropy floor for next-token prediction. Babadi & Sompolinsky give the reason: noise amplification grows as sparseness increases while decorrelation gain grows only logarithmically.
- Copying K = 7 (or any fly constant) into a high-dimensional embedding stage. K_opt ~ 6-7 is the optimum for N ~ 50 correlated glomerular channels. The frequently repeated 'K ~ sqrt(N_in)' extrapolation is not established in Litwin-Kumar et al. 2017 and should be treated as an untested hypothesis, not a design rule.
- Pure delay-and-multiply sequence operators without opponency or a second differently-filtered arm. A correlation detector's output confounds speed with spatial scale (temporal-frequency tuning near 1 Hz regardless of wavelength). Any 'Reichardt-inspired' mixer that multiplies a delayed copy of the same projection by itself inherits exactly this ambiguity; the fly avoids it with opponent subtraction plus downstream spatial pooling, and even then LPTC velocity tuning remains pattern-dependent.
- Treating the lateral horn as a clean innate-valence labelled line. Only ~15 % of tested LH driver lines produce any valence phenotype, ~30 % of LHONs already overlap the mushroom body's DAN/MBON territory, and the quantitative rule by which innate and learned valence are combined in the adult is unmeasured (Dolan et al. 2019; this repo's LITERATURE.md gap list). A 'prior valence head' cannot be justified as established fly biology; it can only be justified as an architecture (a stereotyped low-dimensional pathway alongside a plastic high-dimensional one).
- Surprise/arousal used as a multiplicative GAIN. Phase 2's arousal unit did that and produced nothing. The fly result (Fairhall et al. 2001, Nature 412:787) is that rapid gain rescaling creates ambiguity which is resolved by transmitting the slow variable as a SIGNAL. If surprise is used at all, it must be an input feature, not only a gain.
- Hierarchical Rao-Ballard predictive coding as 'how the fly brain works'. No fly study demonstrates top-down prediction units subtracted from sensory input to yield explicit error units; the 'predictive coding circuit' label applied to the antennal lobe denotes divisive normalisation only, and the genuine fly predictive-coding results are (i) retina/LMC-style spatial prediction via lateral inhibition and (ii) motor efference-copy cancellation. This was already the hidden weakness of phase 2 and should not be re-imported.
- Spike-timing / primacy codes as a near-term route. Primacy coding (rank order of first-responding glomeruli, Wilson, Serrano, Koulakov & Rinberg 2017) is concentration-invariant and attractive, but there is no fly evidence for it and it requires a spiking substrate with calibrated latencies; a rank-order surrogate in a rate model discards exactly the resource that makes it work.

### Open questions

- Does maximising the participation ratio (tr C)^2 / tr(C^2) of an expansion layer's activations on real token data actually predict language-model loss? The fly/cerebellum result is that PR-optimal fan-in matches anatomy; whether PR is the right proxy objective for a 512-4096-dimensional learned embedding stage is untested in either field, and settling it either way is a clean, cheap, publishable result.
- What is the optimal in-degree s and coding level k for a sparse memory layer as a function of input dimension and representation noise? Theory (Babadi-Sompolinsky, Litwin-Kumar) predicts interior optima that move with SNR and with task smoothness; no one has measured the curve at LM scale.
- Why n ~ 1.5 and not n = 2 in the antennal-lobe normalisation? Nothing in the normative theory predicts 1.5. If a learned per-layer exponent in an LM normalisation converges near 1.5 on natural text, that is a striking, testable cross-domain coincidence; if it converges to 2, the fly's value is a biophysical accident.
- Is the two-stage normalisation (per-channel Weber-Fechner adaptation THEN population division) actually better than a single stage in a text setting, where the analogue of 'concentration' is unclear (token frequency? context length? activation norm?)? Kadakia & Emonet show additivity in olfaction (95/85 % vs 70/40 %); the text analogue is undefined and needs to be defined before it can be tested.
- Does explicitly transmitting log(normaliser) forward - the Fairhall 'ambiguity resolution' move - measurably help on non-stationary streams (concatenated documents, topic switches, distribution shift at test time)? This is the single cheapest phase-2 fix and it has a sharp, pre-registrable prediction.
- Does the dual-pathway split (stereotyped low-rank categorical head + sparse high-dimensional plastic memory, both reading one normalised representation) beat a single pathway at matched parameters on continual-learning and one-shot-recall metrics? The fly asserts the architecture; the controlled ML comparison at matched parameters does not exist.
- Is a bilinear sequence operator built from two DIFFERENTLY filtered arms (one low-pass, one band-pass) with an offset suppressive term measurably better than a symmetric gated linear attention at matched parameters? This is the one genuinely under-explored fly motif with clean mathematics (second-order Volterra with asymmetric kernels) and no existing ML analogue that copies the asymmetry.
- What interference/forgetting advantage, if any, do O(k) sparse writes have over dense fast-weight writes at MATCHED write cost rather than matched parameters? Phase 1 compared them on recall accuracy (and lost); interference under continual writing is the metric the biology actually optimises and was never measured.
- Does the anatomical PN->KC sampling bias (Zheng et al. 2022) correspond to anything measurable as an 'ecological prior' that could be re-derived from data statistics rather than copied - i.e. is the right transferable object a bias LEARNED from the input distribution rather than the fly's specific bias?
- Is the reported KC ~ (glomeruli)^(7/2) scaling (Stevens 2015) real, and if so does it imply that the expansion ratio for a d-dimensional input should GROW with d rather than stay at 40x? This single number, if verified, changes the sizing rule for every expansion layer in the design.
- Where exactly does the metabolic argument bite in silicon? The fly's sparseness is forced by ~10^4-10^7 ATP per bit. The silicon analogue is memory bandwidth, not energy per spike, and the two constraints do not have the same optimum - the mapping from 'energy per bit' to 'bytes moved per token' is unworked and determines whether sparse codes are actually the right answer on a GPU.

### Relevance to language models

The olfactory pathway is the one part of the fly brain whose computation is stated as mathematics rather than anatomy, and all of that mathematics is about a problem language models also have: how to turn a moderate-dimensional, correlated, scale-varying input into a high-dimensional code that supports one-shot, local, low-interference writes. The chain is: per-channel adaptive gain (Weber-Fechner, tau ~ 250 ms) -> population divisive normalisation by TOTAL activity (n = 1.5, compressing a 57x concentration range to 6.6x) -> sparse random expansion (50 -> 2,000, fan-in 5-7) -> global feedback inhibition holding ~5 % active -> one-trial plastic readout. Three of those five stages already exist in modern LMs under different names (RMSNorm, sparse memory / top-k latents, auxiliary losses against dead units), which is the strongest available evidence that the motif is real rather than fly-specific: memory-layer LMs beat dense models with more than twice the compute and beat MoE at matched compute and parameters (Berges et al. 2024), and top-k expansion with k ~ 32 works at LLM scale (Gao et al. 2024). What the biology adds that ML currently lacks is (i) a principled objective for choosing fan-in and coding level (participation ratio; the noise-dependent interior optimum in sparseness), (ii) the insistence that normalisation appear twice - once before the threshold and once as scalar feedback after it - which is precisely the dead-latent failure mode of top-k SAEs, (iii) the rule that a front end should transmit derivatives and normalised deviations with a ladder of fixed time constants rather than raw values, and (iv) the requirement, from Fairhall et al. 2001, that whenever you divide by a running statistic you must also transmit that statistic, because adaptation otherwise destroys the information about which distribution you are in. Crucially, none of these is an inductive bias competing with backpropagation at matched parameters - the failure mode that killed phases 1 and 2. They are choices about layer placement, objective functions for hyperparameters, and what gets fed forward. Those survive a learned-gate control by construction, and each yields a pre-registrable prediction with a defined regime (writes per FLOP, interference under continual writing, factual recall, non-stationary streams) in which a state-of-the-art claim is defensible.


## Neuromodulation and internal state as computation in Drosophila: a low-rank, module-addressed control bus over plasticity and timescale — and what transfers to a sequence model

*Key: `neuromodulation-state`.*

### Summary

The fly's neuromodulatory system is small, low-rank and addressed by module. In this repository's own MaleCNS v1.0 data, 541 monoaminergic neurons (0.325 % of 166,700) in 96 cell types reach 20.8 % of the CNS; octopamine has 6.9x the brain-wide mean fan-out. 86 % of the dopaminergic population innervates one structure, the mushroom body, where it is split across ~20 compartments, each served by 1-25 dopaminergic neurons broadcasting one shared signal to that compartment's Kenyon-cell->MBON synapses. The control field therefore has rank of order 100 over 1.7x10^5 neurons. That is the central fact: dopamine is not a global scalar, it is a per-module learning-control bus spent almost entirely on the memory system.

What each channel computes is dissociable. Dopamine sets write, erase and sign: 94 % of its MB output lands presynaptically on KC axons (7,212 vs 486 edges), it potentiates or depresses depending on sub-second order through DopR1/DopR2, and it drives forgetting through DAMB. Serotonin sets the coincidence time window, via a local KC->DPM->KC loop. Octopamine sets global sensory gain (~2x during flight). Internal states - hunger, thirst, sleep need - are slow leaky integrators (minutes to hours) that change receptor levels per compartment, invert the behavioural sign of readouts, and, for sleep, flip a genuinely bistable dFB switch that gates an offline consolidation phase replaying the same dopaminergic neurons that encoded.

The decisive engineering point is mathematical. RMSNorm(g*x) = RMSNorm(x) exactly, so a global gain upstream of a norm carries zero information; a global scalar on the loss cancels under any preconditioned optimizer. This is why Phase 2's arousal was inert, and it is a theorem, not a tuning failure. The same bounded scalar applied to a decay gate moves the memory horizon from 800 to 50 tokens and applied to logits moves entropy by 5.4 nats. Neuromodulation must act on plasticity and timescale.

Two cautions. Phase 1's wiring null is what a neuromodulation-literate reading predicts (Bargmann 2012; Marder 2012): the connectome is the substrate, the modulatory state is the circuit. And this repo's data have the modulatory system silently zeroed - every monoaminergic edge has weight 0, and W_dan_kc/W_dan_mbon export as all-zero arrays. Fix that before phase 3 reads them.

### Established (with sources)

- The anatomical connectome is a lower bound on the circuit; neuromodulators reconfigure which functional circuit the same wiring implements. This is the canonical statement of the field (Bargmann 2012, BioEssays 34(6):458-465; Bargmann & Marder 2013, Nature Methods 10:483-490; Marder 2012, Neuron 76(1):1-11, from the ~30-neuron crustacean stomatogastric ganglion where one wiring diagram produces many distinct motor patterns under different modulators). A neuroscientist reading FlyBrainLLM's Phase-1 null ('wiring == degree-preserving shuffle') would not be surprised: wiring without the modulatory state is the part of the circuit that carries the least function.
- The fly's monoaminergic system is tiny and broadcast-shaped. Computed directly from this repository's MaleCNS v1.0 data (data/malecns, 166,700 neurons, 10,520,431 edges): 392 dopaminergic + 101 octopaminergic + 48 serotonergic = 541 neurons = 0.325 % of the CNS, in 96 distinct cell types (44 DA, 38 OA, 14 5-HT). They contact 34,700 distinct postsynaptic neurons = 20.8 % of the CNS. Mean out-degree: octopamine 434, serotonin 216, dopamine 138, against a brain-wide mean of 63 (octopamine is 6.9x the mean fan-out). Transmitter identities are EM-image predictions (Eckstein, Bates et al. 2024, Cell 187(10):2574-2594; reported per-type accuracy ~90 % FAFB / 85 % hemibrain for dopaminergic, 85 %/100 % for octopaminergic), so these counts carry prediction error.
- Dopamine in the fly is not a global scalar: it is spent almost entirely on the memory system and is addressed per compartment. Of the 392 predicted dopaminergic neurons in MaleCNS v1.0, 316 are PAM-cluster and 22 PPL-cluster, i.e. ~86 % innervate the mushroom body. The adult MB is divided into 15 compartments tiled by 21 MBON types and 20 DAN types (Aso et al. 2014, eLife 3:e04577); each DAN type projects to one, or at most two, compartments. In the repo's left-hemisphere MB extraction the DANs carry 21 distinct compartment labels with 1-25 DANs each (gamma4: 25 DANs, gamma5: 23, beta2: 16, beta'2m: 14).
- Compartments are independent learning units with different rules. Aso & Rubin 2016 (eLife 5:e16135) compared memories written by optogenetic activation of individual DAN cell types and found extensive differences between compartments in training requirement, decay dynamics, storage capacity and flexibility to overwrite; a single DAN type can write an aversive memory, reduce one, or write an appetitive memory depending on when it fires relative to the odour. This is a bank of memory modules with per-module learning rate, per-module forgetting rate and per-module valence, not one memory with one learning rate.
- The sign of plasticity is set by sub-second relative timing through two dopamine receptors with opposite second messengers. Handler et al. 2019 (Cell 178(1):60-75) show that shifting the relative timing of odour and dopaminergic reinforcement by less than 1 s switches the valence of the memory; DopR1 and DopR2 couple to distinct second messengers and direct synaptic depression or potentiation respectively, with DopR2/Galphaq signalling strictly dependent on the KC-then-DAN vs DAN-then-KC order. A modulator here is a signed, temporally asymmetric gate, not a positive scalar.
- Dopamine acts on two timescales at once in the same synapse: acute presynaptic gain and lasting plasticity, both compartment-specific. Cohn, Morantte & Ruta 2015 (Cell 163(7):1742-1755) show dopaminergic input modulates KC->MBON transmission with spatial specificity so that one KC conveys its odour signal differently to each postsynaptic compartment, and that the DANs form an interconnected network encoding external context and internal state. Structurally this is a presynaptic (key-side) modulation: in the repo's MB extraction, DAN->KC edges outnumber DAN->MBON edges 7,212 to 486 (left) and 6,177 to 478 (right) - ~94 % of the DAN output inside the MB lands on Kenyon-cell axons, i.e. it multiplies the write term, not the read-out activation.
- The teaching signal is generated by the network itself: MBONs feed back onto DANs. In the repo's MaleCNS MB extraction, MBON->DAN feedback is 339 edges / 2,861 contacts (left) and 300 / 2,605 (right). Eschbach et al. 2020 (Nature Neuroscience 23:544-555) mapped the full input connectome of larval DANs, found a large population of MBON->DAN feedback neurons linking aversive and appetitive systems, and showed by simulation that these feedback motifs increase model flexibility and learning performance. Bennett, Philippides & Nowotny 2021 (Nature Communications 12:2569) show the loop yields reinforcement-prediction-error learning. The fly's 'error gate' is a small learned recurrent critic, not a fixed formula.
- Dopamine also drives forgetting, through a different receptor. Sabandal, Berry & Davis 2021 (Nature 591:426-430) identify a single dopaminergic neuron whose activation transiently suppresses retrieval of an intact long-term memory, acting through the DAMB receptor in a restricted compartment of the KC axons; transient and permanent forgetting share the dopaminergic mechanism and receptor but differ in circuit and downstream signalling. Write and erase are driven by the same transmitter through different receptors.
- Serotonin sets the coincidence time window - the eligibility-trace length - not the gain. Zeng et al. 2023 (Neuron 111(7):1118-1135) show that suppressing or promoting 5-HT signalling respectively shortens or prolongs the coincidence window for odour-shock learning and for KC synaptic plasticity, via a local loop: KC-released acetylcholine activates nicotinic receptors on the DPM neuron, which releases 5-HT back onto KCs through the 5-HT1a receptor. A modulator that controls a temporal binding window is a different computational object from one that controls amplitude.
- Octopamine is the arousal/gain channel, and it is the one that behaves like a global multiplier. Maimon, Straw & Dickinson 2010 (Nature Neuroscience 13:393-399) showed peak-to-peak responses of vertical-system visual interneurons roughly double during flight versus rest; Suver, Mamiya & Dickinson 2012 (Current Biology 22(24):2294-2302) showed octopaminergic neurons become active during flight and that exogenous octopamine reproduces the gain increase. Aimon et al. 2019 (PLoS Biology 17(2):e2006732) show a global increase in brain activity during walking versus rest, with only a small local increase during grooming.
- Hunger reconfigures the mushroom body and can invert the behavioural sign of a readout. Tsao et al. 2018 (eLife 7:e35264) found that starvation bidirectionally modulates MBON responses to food odours at the KC->MBON synapse, mediated by six types of dopaminergic neurons. Perisse et al. 2016 (Neuron 90(5):1086-1099) showed aversive learning and appetitive motivation toggle alternate modes of one feed-forward inhibitory pathway (MBON-gamma1pedc>alpha/beta, 'MVP2') to produce either conditioned avoidance or approach. Krashes et al. 2009 (Cell 139(2):416-427) showed hunger acts via NPF to inhibit the MP1/PPL1-gamma1pedc dopaminergic neurons and thereby release appetitive memory expression. The same stored association is read with opposite behavioural sign depending on state.
- State changes the receptor, not only the transmitter. A 2025 study using endogenous split-GFP receptor tagging mapped Dop1R1 and Dop2R across all 15 MB lobe compartments under hunger and thirst and found state-specific, compartment-specific changes in receptor level (hunger raises Dop1R1 across alpha/beta and Dop2R across alpha'/beta'; thirst raises both in gamma and lowers Dop2R in alpha/beta) with no significant change in baseline dopamine release (PubMed 42284989; venue/author list uncertain - verify before citing). Modulatory sensitivity is a per-module state variable.
- Internal states are implemented as slow leaky integrators with persistence far outlasting their trigger. Jung et al. 2020 (Neuron 105(2):322-333) showed pCd neurons act as a leaky integrator that maintains a social-arousal state for minutes after the P1 trigger ends, and that inhibiting them changes the integrator's leak rate constant. Anderson 2016 (Nature Reviews Neuroscience 17:692-704) formalises internal states by their primitives: scalability, persistence, valence, generalisation. Deutsch et al. 2020 (eLife 9:e59502) show a persistent internal state in females with similar properties.
- Sleep pressure is a homeostatic integrator read out by a bistable switch. Pimentel et al. 2016 (Nature 536:333-337) showed dorsal fan-shaped body (dFB) neurons switch between electrically active (high input resistance, long membrane time constant) and electrically silent states as a function of sleep need, and that the switch is operated by dopamine through antagonistic modulation of voltage-dependent and voltage-independent potassium channels. Donlea et al. 2014 (Neuron 81(4):860-872) showed sleep pressure is transduced into dFB excitability via the Rho-GAP crossveinless-c; Donlea et al. 2018 (Neuron 97(2):378-389) described the recurrent circuitry balancing sleep need and sleep. This is a discrete mode variable driven by an accumulator, not a continuous gain.
- Offline consolidation reuses the same dopaminergic machinery that did the encoding. Dag et al. 2019 (eLife 8:e42786) showed that consolidation of courtship long-term memory is mediated by reactivation during post-learning sleep of the same dopaminergic neurons (DAN-aSP13, acting via DopR1 on gamma Kenyon cells) that were involved in acquisition, and that specific fan-shaped-body neurons induce the sleep that permits it. Haynes, Christmann & Griffith 2015 (eLife 4:e03868) link a single pair of neurons to sleep-dependent consolidation. Replay in the fly is tagged by the modulator, not uniform.
- Plasticity is gated by the circadian clock through a neuropeptide. Flyer-Adams et al. 2020 (J Neurosci 40(47):9066-9077) showed that appetitive olfactory short-term memory requires Pigment-dispersing factor (PDF) signalling and its receptor PDFR, with a larger requirement in the morning than in the evening, and that the clock circuit stabilises appetitive memory across the day. Learning rate has a slow exogenous clock on top of its endogenous error-driven control.
- Neuropeptides form a second, 'wireless' connectivity layer that the synaptic connectome does not contain. Drosophila has roughly 50 neuropeptide precursor genes and a comparable number of peptide GPCRs (Nassel & Zandawala 2019, Progress in Neurobiology 179:101607; a FlyBase FB2024_02 curated list gives 51 neuropeptide-encoding genes). Monoamines and peptides act substantially by volume transmission (extrasynaptic release from varicosities, micrometre range for monoamines, longer for peptides), so their influence is not readable from synaptic adjacency. Only two Drosophila peptidergic networks have been mapped as networks so far (neurosecretory cells and clock neurons, both 2024).
- States are not simply 'gain up / gain down': circuits actively compensate to preserve function across states.
